Balance of payments and exchange rate: FX is supported, but increasingly expensive 
The dram has continued to show above-expected resilience to global capital volatility in 
2026, as it is supported by rising capital inflows, remittances, comfortable reserves and 
favourable investor sentiment linked to the peace process with Azerbaijan and positive 
rating trajectory. This continues to offset Armenia’s sizeable current account deficit, 
helping keep the currency stronger than implied by long-term valuation metrics.  
However, Armenia remains dependent on continued external flows, while close 
economic links to Russia remain vulnerabilities, even if the recent trade data points to a 
reduction in Russia-Armenia trade. Recent EU trade liberalisation covers around 80% of 
Armenian exports to the bloc. The immediate macro impact is likely to be limited, 
however, as the EU currently accounts for only around 12% of Armenia’s goods exports. 
We therefore expect the AMD to stay resilient to a potential global risk off in the near 
term but see limited room for further appreciation and continue to view longer-term 
risks as skewed towards gradual depreciation. 
Fig 18 Armenia: Trade balance level and composition  Fig 19 Armenia: Balance of payments vs AMD

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Sovereign credit strategy 
This year has seen a continuation of fundamental improvement for Armenia, with 
positive rating outlooks at all three agencies, and elections that passed without major 
surprises. While there remains the potential for further positive catalysts from the 
ongoing peace process with Azerbaijan, conflict in the Middle East leaves Armenia in a 
more vulnerable position, given the increasing reliance on external inflows.

Economic overview  
Azerbaijan remains the CCA region’s financially strongest sovereign, supported by 
large and growing fiscal and monetary reserve assets, low public debt and a robust 
external balance sheet. Higher hydrocarbon prices have strengthened the near-
term fiscal and external outlook and supported confidence in the non-oil economy. 
However, the strength of the sovereign balance sheet contrasts with a much less 
impressive underlying growth story; declining oil production, limited diversification 
and subdued private investment continue to cap potential growth at around 2-3%. 
Inflation remains manageable for now, but imported price pressures warrant caution. 
We therefore remain constructive on sovereign resilience and AZN stability, while 
seeing the ability to convert hydrocarbon wealth into a deeper pool of private 
investment and investable non-oil assets as the main medium-term challenge. 
Economic activity: Recovering now, but long-term growth limited 
GDP growth is currently into the second year of below-average growth, dragged down 
by an underperforming fuel sector at the beginning of the year, reflecting both 
structural and temporary factors. At the same time, higher hydrocarbon prices support 
financial inflows, which pass through to the wider economy, supporting confidence in 
the non-fuel sectors. Retail trade and non-fuel industry output have been accelerating 
throughout 2026 amid faster lending activity.  
While the consolidated budget remains in surplus thanks to fuel revenues, the stable 
non-oil deficit supports domestic demand. Following the extended fuel-led slowdown of 
economic activity in 2025-26, we expect growth to recover in the medium term. 
However, the potential growth rate appears capped at 2-3% unless economic 
diversification accelerates. 
Fig 20 Azerbaijan: GDP growth by key contributors  Fig 21 Azerbaijan: GDP growth by sectors

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Household spending relies increasingly on retail credit, as positive real income growth is 
insufficient to cover consumption. On the corporate side, the fuel sector still weighs on 
industrial growth, but the recent rise in corporate lending suggests investment appetite 
elsewhere may recover.

Budget policy is benefiting from higher oil price environment 
Azerbaijan’s fiscal stance remains robust, though not growth restrictive. On the revenue 
side, fuel receipts are broadly stable, as lower export volumes are being offset by higher 
prices. Non-fuel revenues are also largely stable. On the expenditure side, higher social 
and defence spending, alongside persistently high investment outlays, continue to 
underpin spending pressures. The war in neighbouring Iran creates upside risks to 
defence and security spending.  
As a result, the consolidated budget remains in a comfortable surplus of around 2% of 
GDP thanks to fuel revenues, while non-fuel budget is in stable c.10% GDP deficit. 
Azerbaijan’s fuel fundamentals remain solid, with annualised revenues per US$1/bbl of 
oil price at US$150-200 million. This has helped keep the fiscal breakeven oil price stable 
at around US$59/bbl last year, even as spending has increased. Even assuming some 
increase in the fiscal breakeven in the coming years due to continued spending growth, 
ING’s house view on oil prices suggests that Azerbaijan will remain in fiscal surplus on a 
three-year horizon. 
For investors, the bigger issue is asset creation. Strong public finances limit sovereign 
issuance, leaving the investment story dependent on new non-oil projects and private-
sector borrowers.  
Fig 24 Azerbaijan: Consolidated budget balance  Fig 25 Azerbaijan: Budget sensitivities to oil prices

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: CPI risks manageable, rates on hold 
Inflation risks have recently shifted somewhat to the upside, as food prices and higher 
global transport and commodity costs have renewed pressure on Azerbaijan’s producer 
price index. However, the exchange-rate peg remains a strong nominal anchor, limiting 
pass-through to consumer prices. With CPI still within the 4±2% target range, we expect 
the refinancing rate to remain on hold over the medium term.

However, further imported inflation linked to developments in the Middle East is the

Fig 26 Azerbaijan: Inflation and key rate  Fig 27 Azerbaijan: Inflation drivers

Balance of payments and AZN: External buffers reinforced by oil 
As on the fiscal side, higher oil prices have materially improved the external outlook, 
reversing much of the deterioration seen in 2025. As each US$10/bbl of oil price adds 
annual exports of around 4% of GDP, in 2026F the current account surplus may reach 
12% of GDP. In the meantime, sovereign assets equivalent to 115% of GDP keep growing 
and continue to provide substantial protection against potential external shocks. These 
buffers remain the main support for the AZN peg and broader macro stability.  
Over the longer term, however, declining hydrocarbon production and limited export 
diversification are likely to gradually erode the external surplus. Azerbaijan remains one 
of the region’s strongest external stories, although commodity dependence continues to 
shape its medium-term outlook. 
Fig 28 Azerbaijan: 12M exports by region  Fig 29 Azerbaijan: External sovereign asset position

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Sovereign credit strategy  
The fundamental picture is generally positive for Azerbaijan, with ratings now 
comfortably in the IG space and Moody’s holding a positive outlook. At the same time, 
the country stands to be a significant beneficiary from the current energy price spike, 
given its high sensitivity to oil prices in terms of fiscal and external accounts. The 
ongoing peace process with Armenia seems more likely to act as a positive catalyst at 
this stage, given the progress in recent years.

Economic overview 
Kazakhstan continues to benefit from one of the strongest sovereign and external 
positions in the CCA region, supported by sizeable sovereign assets, a balanced 
network of international partners and higher oil prices. Strong portfolio inflows, 
attractive real rates, progress towards Euroclear connectivity, growing domestic 
debt supply and positive rating momentum have supported the tenge and local 
markets, while a sizeable backlog of future state FX sales provides an additional 
buffer against market stress. 
However, these strengths coexist with persistent current-account deficits, continued 
fiscal and quasi-fiscal stimulus, limited private sector investment and an uneven 
track record in fiscal discipline and inflation targeting. Greater reliance on portfolio 
inflows is also making the tenge more sensitive to changes in global risk appetite. 
This creates a growing trade-off between the benefits of deeper foreign 
participation in the local market and greater sensitivity to shifts in global 
positioning. Current inflation trends leave room for moderate monetary easing, but 
domestic fiscal and quasi-fiscal stimulus alongside external price risks should keep 
the easing cycle gradual. 
Economic activity: growth resilient despite oil disruptions and VAT hike 
GDP growth has slowed this year compared to the exceptional pace seen in 2025 but 
remains robust despite the VAT hike and oil-related disruptions. Disruptions to oil 
production and exports appear to have been the main drag on growth in 1H26 and 
remain the main risk for 2H26.  
Non-oil sectors continue to perform relatively well, while higher oil prices support 
confidence and improve the fiscal and external outlook. Domestic demand also appears 
supported by quasi-fiscal stimulus estimated at 4-5% of GDP. At the same time, retail 
and corporate lending are showing signs of cooling amid tight monetary conditions. We 
see GDP growth remaining in the 4.5-5.0% range in the coming years. 
Fig 30 Kazakhstan: GDP growth dynamics and structure  Fig 31 Kazakhstan: Key oil sector indicators

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Budget policy: Consolidation ongoing 
Headline fiscal policy has entered a consolidation phase, reflected in lower reliance on 
National Fund (NFRK) transfers to finance current spending, although a durable track 
record of fiscal discipline has yet to be established.

In addition to the quasi-fiscal stimulus provided through Baiterek lending, during 2026, 
the three-year state spending plan increased by KZT6.5tr to be carried out through 
targeted transfers from the National Fund (NFRK). This fits the previous pattern of 
spending plans being revised upwards over time. At the same time, even with the 
additional spending, the overall volume of National Fund spending, including guaranteed 
and targeted transfers, is still expected to decline, in our estimates, to 1.5-2.5% of GDP 
over the medium term.  
Importantly, however, this should have little effect on the headline republican (central 
government) budget deficit, which should remain in the 2-3% GDP range, determining 
the annual net debt issuance requirement. The consolidated budget should gravitate 
around balance but can also be subject to volatility due to the large role of NFRK’s 
investment income, which reached 4% of GDP in 2025. 
Fig 32 Kazakhstan: Republican budget balance  Fig 33 Kazakhstan: Consolidated budget balance

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: Real rate to remain high 
The moderate easing in inflation from its 2025 peak and a resilient tenge have allowed 
the NBK to start cutting the base rate in 2026. Inflation pressures from the VAT hike and 
global commodity-price spike have remained under control for now, while portfolio 
inflows and attractive real yields have given an additional boost to the currency. At the 
same time, tariff increases, fiscal and quasi-fiscal stimulus, and producer-price pressures 
amid global inflation risks continue to justify our above-consensus CPI outlook.  
Therefore, we expect the easing cycle to continue only gradually, while remaining alert 
to potential external inflation shocks. Although Kazakhstan retains room for nominal 
easing, real policy rates are likely to remain high by regional standards and supportive 
of capital inflows.

Balance of payments and exchange rate: Portfolio flows increasingly matter 
Higher oil prices have strengthened Kazakhstan's external position, although part of the 
benefit is offset by weaker export volumes. Despite some recent improvement, the 
country continues to run structural current account deficits as strong imports and 
dividend outflows offset export revenues.  
Fig 36 Kazakhstan: Fuel export volume and value  Fig 37 Kazakhstan: Balance of payment composition

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

As a result, capital flows are becoming increasingly important for KZT dynamics. 
Progress towards Euroclear connectivity, positive rating momentum and additional 
public and corporate debt issuance should continue attracting portfolio inflows amid 
high real rates, supporting the tenge. Kazakhstan's capital market modernisation is also 
broadening beyond international settlement access, with the NBK now experimenting 
with tokenised debt issuance alongside the expanding digital tenge infrastructure. We 
have improved our KZT outlook but note that greater reliance on portfolio capital in an 
economy with persistent twin deficits also increases sensitivity to shifts in global risk 
appetite and flow reversals.  
The good news, however, is that the government has recently accumulated a backlog of 
FX sales linked to National Fund spending, which we estimate at c.US$2bn for June-
October. An unwinding of that backlog could provide an additional buffer for the 
domestic FX market in the event of external stress.

Sovereign credit strategy 
Overall, the underlying fundamental momentum is generally positive, with fiscal 
consolidation under close focus, while higher energy prices should offer a modest boost 
to export and budget revenues. However, as with budget policy, on the issuance front, 
more quasi-sovereign supply remains a risk in the coming years given low spread levels 
compared to most BBB peers.

Economic overview  
Kyrgyzstan is the newest addition to our CCA coverage and arguably the region’s 
most striking growth story. Economic growth remains exceptionally strong, 
supported by construction and trade activity, infrastructure investment, remittance 
inflows and rapid credit growth. Public finances remain relatively comfortable and 
international reserves have risen sharply, while the stability of the som has helped 
insulate the economy from external price pressures. 
However, the current pace of growth also points to significant overheating risks. 
Inflation is already well above the central bank’s target range, credit expansion 
remains rapid and real rates offer little evidence of tight monetary conditions, while 
the recent slowdown in corporate deposit inflows points to greater volatility in the 
corporate funding base. Large external deficits, dependence on Russia-related 
remittances and unusually opaque external flows further complicate the picture. 
The recent sovereign rating improvement is encouraging, but Kyrgyzstan remains 
an early-stage catch-up story in which macro credibility is still lagging the headline 
growth. 
Economic activity: Exceptional growth, increasingly demand-led 
Despite recent moderation to 9.7% YoY as of 3Q26, economic growth in Kyrgyzstan 
remains exceptionally strong at 12.3% YoY on a rolling four-quarter basis, with trade and 
construction alone contributing around 9.6 percentage points. Construction has become 
a significantly more important growth engine since 2022, supported by infrastructure 
investment, while strong trade activity continues to reflect both domestic consumption 
and Kyrgyzstan's role in regional transit trade. Industrial performance, by contrast, 
remains more volatile. 
The composition of growth makes the current double-digit pace unlikely to be sustained 
indefinitely. Some of the exceptional post-2022 boost from regional trade rerouting 
should gradually fade, while activity is increasingly reliant on domestic investment and 
consumption. Large infrastructure projects should keep growth relatively strong, but the 
challenge is to convert the recent acceleration into broader productivity gains rather 
than a prolonged demand boom.

Fiscal policy: Headline surpluses mask a growing investment push 
Kyrgyzstan’s consolidated budget remains comfortably in surplus by 2.5-3.0% of GDP, 
supported by rapid economic growth and strong revenues, but the headline balance 
increasingly understates the expansionary direction of fiscal policy. Direct expenditures 
have been growing faster than GDP-driven tax revenues, largely reflecting the state’s 
infrastructure and investment push, as spending on ‘economic policies’ in the fiscal 
statistics is currently almost 5 percentage points of GDP higher than two years ago, 
eroding the underlying balance of tax revenues and direct expenditures. More 
importantly, once volatile non-tax revenues associated with financial and capital 
transactions are excluded, the fiscal position moves into a widening deficit. This helps 
explain why government borrowing has continued to increase despite the headline 
surplus. 
Nominal public debt has more than doubled from KGS436bn in 2021 to around 
KGS900bn currently, even as rapid nominal GDP growth has brought the debt ratio down 
sharply and more recently kept it broadly stable at around 40% of GDP. Recent 
borrowing, including the recent US$700m Eurobond placement in 2025, reflects 
primarily government financial transactions, including operations involving capital 
injections into state-owned enterprises, as suggested by the recent IMF analysis. The 
growing role of such operations means that the composition and use of new borrowing 
are becoming increasingly important for assessing Kyrgyzstan’s underlying fiscal 
position and sovereign credit story. 
Part of this borrowing reflects an infrastructure-heavy growth model, including the CKU 
railway and Kambarata-1. This raises potential growth if successfully executed, but also 
makes project execution, SOE balance sheets and the structure of external funding 
increasingly important for sovereign investors. 
Fig 42 Kyrgyzstan: Consolidated budget balance   Fig 43 Kyrgyzstan: Key public debt metrics

Source: National sources, IMF, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: Overheating sends hawkish signals 
Inflation has accelerated well beyond the National Bank's 5-7% target range. Domestic 
tariff increases were an important contributor in the first half of 2026, but price 
pressures have subsequently broadened, with strong demand adding to external food 
and other cost pressures. The relative stability of the som has provided an important 
offset to imported inflation. 
This leaves monetary policy in a difficult position. With inflation broadly matching the 
12% policy rate, monetary conditions do not look particularly tight. Rapid money and 
credit growth and abundant liquidity also point to weak transmission. We expect further 
near-term tightening, followed by scope for partial reversal during 2027 if inflation 
moderates. We also think stronger liquidity management, alongside the policy rate, will 
be important if the National Bank is to bring inflation sustainably back into its target 
range.

Balance of payments and KGS: Strong buffers, vulnerable external accounts 
Som stability masks a much more complicated external position. Kyrgyzstan runs a 
structurally large merchandise trade deficit, currently at US$11bn on a 12-month rolling 
basis, increasingly driven by machinery and other imports from China and other non-CIS 
economies. Some of these imports reflect the domestic investment boom, while part of 
the exceptional trade expansion has been related to regional re-exports. The latter 
appears to have peaked in 2024-2025 and has normalised since then. Meanwhile, trade 
ties with Russia remain deep, with bilateral trade exceeding US$4bn per annum, supporting 
near-term activity but also reinforcing Kyrgyzstan's exposure to Russia-related external risks. 
Remittance inflows provide an important counterweight to the trade deficit. Gross 
inflows remain close to record levels of US$3bn per annum and continue to be 
dominated by Russia (90%), while net remittances of around 11% of GDP provide 
substantial support to household incomes, domestic demand and the FX market. This 
provides a large buffer but leaves Kyrgyzstan heavily exposed to economic and policy 
developments in Russia. 
Fig 46 Kyrgyzstan: Trade balance by region  Fig 47 Kyrgyzstan: Net remittances

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Official BOP data requires particular caution; IMF estimates suggest that unrecorded  
re-exports materially distort the measured current account deficit. Structural current 
account deficits of US$5-7bn per annum require continued external financing, while 
large unrecorded trade and financial flows make the headline current account numbers 
unusually difficult to interpret. FDI and, more recently, portfolio flows have helped 
finance the external funding gap. International reserves have risen sharply in nominal 
terms, but much of the increase reflects gold revaluation; physical gold holdings have 
remained broadly stable. 
For now, these buffers and the managed exchange rate regime should continue 
ensuring som stability, seen since mid-2025. But the combination of a large trade deficit,

Banking sector: Rapid financial deepening brings its own risks 
Loan growth in Kyrgyzstan remains very strong, particularly in retail, which is expanding 
rapidly from a relatively low base, though the pace has recently moderated. Corporate 
loan growth is accelerating and catching up with retail, with FX lending playing a stable 
role. Meanwhile, deposit and broader funding growth has slowed. Most notably, 
corporate deposit inflows slowed materially, and this stands out compared to other 
segments of the banking sector. 
The initial drop appears partly related to large corporate and government payments in the 
middle of the year, but the subsequent weakness may also reflect tighter sanctions 
compliance, including on the A7 payment network and the ruble-linked A757 
cryptocurrency, as well as new restrictions and fees affecting non-resident corporate 
accounts.   
On the lending side, the low starting point means that part of the expansion represents 
welcome financial deepening. However, rapid credit creation is also reinforcing domestic 
demand and weakening monetary policy transmission at a time when inflation is 
already high. Credit quality, bank liquidity and the pace at which lending continues to 
outgrow the funding base are therefore becoming increasingly important macro-
financial indicators to watch. 
Fig 50 Kyrgyzstan: Bank funding indicators  Fig 51 Kyrgyzstan: Bank lending indicators

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Sovereign credit strategy 
While Kyrgyzstan remains an interesting reform story for EM investors, recent 
performance has seen spreads squeeze tight versus peers. Headline fiscal metrics look 
strong but mask increased spending and borrowing needs, which could drive further

### Page 26

CCA macro and credit November 2026

26

Economic overview 
Uzbekistan remains our preferred medium-term CCA growth and reform story, but 
strong recent performance also brings increasing signs of overheating and leaves 
important macro imbalances unresolved. Growth remains well above potential, 
inflation is still above target and persistent twin deficits coexist with continued high 
state involvement in the economy and a much-reduced liquid component of the 
sovereign wealth fund. At the same time, reform and privatisation momentum, high 
real rates and strong capital inflows have supported the soum, while unusually 
restrained physical gold exports leave potential for additional FX supply if sales 
normalise. 
We therefore retain a stronger-than-consensus medium-term view on UZS and see 
scope for gradual monetary easing, but a durable break from its historical 
depreciation trend requires further disinflation, fiscal discipline and evidence that 
private capital inflows can remain strong as the current reform and privatisation 
push matures. 
Economic activity: Accelerating beyond potential 
Uzbekistan’s GDP growth has been exceptionally strong recently, supported by domestic 
demand catching up from a low base. While activity is expected to moderate from the 
current above-potential levels, Uzbekistan should remain one of the fastest-growing 
economies in emerging markets. Strong investment spending, industrial activity and 
ongoing reforms continue to support expansion, while positive demographic trends 
provide an additional tailwind.  
A gradual slowdown in the GDP growth is likely to reflect normalisation in the activity 
trends, with growth remaining comfortably above regional peers. The immediate 
challenge is managing overheating as consumption and investment continue to grow 
rapidly.  
Fig 53 Uzbekistan: GDP growth composition  Fig 54 Uzbekistan: Key activity indicators

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Fiscal policy: Pace of consolidation moderated 
Fiscal consolidation is continuing, with the general government deficit narrowing to 1.1% 
of GDP in the four quarters to 3Q26, from 4.1% in 2023. The pace of improvement has 
slowed recently, partly because unusually low gold exports have constrained revenues, 
which we expect to be temporary. At the same time, expenditure growth is catching up 
with revenue growth, pointing to a more structural challenge for Uzbekistan’s fiscal policy.

### Page 27

The government also has strong incentives to maintain fiscal restraint. Domestic 
demand is already running hot, while several years of budget deficits have reduced the 
UFRD's liquid component from 17% of GDP in 2019 to around 3% in 2025. We see fiscal 
deficits limited to around 1% of GDP on the three-year horizon, with a growing share of 
debt financing and privatisation as a source of financing, which should be taken 
positively by portfolio investors. 
The privatisation programme, though stemming largely from the fiscal needs, is gaining 
importance as a capital-markets story. Following the UzNIF transaction, the widening 
pipeline of state assets creates scope for international capital raising. 
Fig 55 Uzbekistan: Consolidated budget balance   Fig 56 Uzbekistan: Sovereign fund composition

Source: National sources, IMF, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, IMF, Fitch, CEIC, ING

Inflation and monetary policy: Scope for gradual easing 
Over the past couple of years, inflation in Uzbekistan has moderated substantially from 
previous highs, aided by the recent fiscal consolidation, and this has created room for a 
cautious monetary policy easing cycle. However, current CPI remains above the central 
bank's long-term objective, while strong domestic demand, administrative tariff 
adjustments and global food and commodity risks argue against aggressive rate cuts.  
We therefore expect the central bank to deliver only gradual easing from a still-
restrictive level. Real rates remain relatively attractive and should continue to support 
macro stability while allowing policymakers to balance growth objectives against the 
need for further disinflation. 
Fig 57 Uzbekistan: Inflation, expectations, and key rate  Fig 58 Uzbekistan: Inflation drivers

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, UN FAO, ING

Balance of payments and exchange rate: Capital inflows support UZS 
Our stronger-than-consensus medium-term view on the soum continues to be 
reinforced by recent developments. High real rates, gold prices, an expected catch-up in 
physical gold exports and growing foreign participation in Uzbekistan's privatisation 
programme provide important support for the balance of payments and FX market. The 
investment pipeline continues to broaden geographically, with a recently announced

package of US-linked projects potentially worth US$12bn upon implementation, spanning 
transport, critical minerals, infrastructure and financial services. This should underpin the 
resilience of the UZS despite the country's persistent trade and current-account deficits.  
Uzbekistan’s physical gold exports have been significantly restrained this year, at just 18 
tonnes in January-September 2026 compared with 85 tonnes in the same period of 
2025. This means that the benefit from the gold price rally has so far been limited on the 
external trade side. The slight appreciation of the soum against major currencies this 
year appears to have been driven largely by capital inflows, partly supported by high 
real rates, as has been the case in Kazakhstan. 
Fig 59 Uzbekistan: CBRU monthly gold exports  Fig 60 Uzbekistan: Balance of payments

Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

The reason for the unusually low sales has not been formally explained. However, the 
simultaneous increase in the central bank's physical gold holdings – to 453.5 tonnes in 
October - indicates deliberate retention amid continued extraction. The significant 
restraint on gold exports so far this year suggests some upside potential for the soum if 
export volumes recover towards their historical range of 80-120 tonnes per year. This 
catch-up in delayed gold exports is one of the factors we see supporting the currency 
over the medium term. 
However, the longer-term outlook is less certain, as Uzbekistan continues to run sizeable 
twin deficits, while inflation remains above target. As a result, we view the UZS positively 
in the medium-term but believe a durable break from the historical depreciation trend 
would require sustained progress in reducing macro imbalances and deepening 
structural reforms. 
Sovereign credit strategy 
The fundamental picture for Uzbekistan has improved significantly in recent years, with 
narrowing twin deficits amid solid growth reflected in rating upgrades. Fitch’s positive 
outlook suggests there is still scope for improvement to continue, while the stronger 
credit profile should increase the country’s resilience to the current energy shock. An 
expected catch-up in gold exports, alongside ongoing capital inflows linked to the 
privatisation pipeline should keep the momentum strong, and we see the potential for 
more fundamental upside.

## Document 326: Cyber Glitches, Bootlegged Chips & Deflating China

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cyber Glitches, Bootlegged Chips & Deflating China.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-17
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Cyber Glitches, Bootlegged Chips & Deflating China
Date:    8 hours ago
Cyber Glitches, Bootlegged Chips & Deflating China

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Cyber Glitches, Bootlegged
Chips & Deflating China
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 16 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Tonik / Unsplash

I. Cyber Glitches & Chip Controls
"Once is happenstance. Twice is coincidence. Three times is enemy
action." That's according to Ian Fleming’s Goldfinger (1959)

### Page 2

What, then, should we make of the recent string of technology
disruptions? In October, hackers tampered with freezers at roughly 700
French supermarkets, spoiling food and triggering fires. Last Thursday,
Microsoft suffered a 10-hour outage affecting Outlook and Teams. On
Friday, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) imposed the longest
nationwide flight ground stop since 9/11. Then, today, customers at major
US banks, including JPMorgan Chase, reported problems accessing their

accounts, while a computer failure temporarily grounded United Airlines
flights.
France blamed Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps for the
supermarket attacks, although Tehran denies responsibility. The other
disruptions appear to have been technical failures, including software
bugs at Microsoft and JPMorgan Chase and a malfunctioning router at the
FAA. United's outage was resolved within 90 minutes. No evidence links
these incidents, but their timing is curious, to say the least.

Regulatory loopholes might also be considered glitches, and one may
finally be getting fixed. On Monday, the US Commerce Department
announced tighter restrictions to prevent Chinese companies from
accessing American AI chips and computing power through overseas data
centers, particularly in Southeast Asia. The measures follow scrutiny of
Nvidia's ties to Singapore-based Megaspeed, which has been accused of
circumventing US export controls.
The announcement added to the semiconductor stock selloff as investors

worried about lost chip sales and slower overseas data center investment.
The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index (SOX) plunged 5.3% today, while
the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX) fell 5.4%. Nvidia declined 4.1%,
AMD 5.6%, and Micron more than 7%. The Nasdaq Composite dropped
2.5%, remaining below its 200-day moving average (charts).

### Page 3

Investors are particularly worried about Nvidia. How much of its revenue
could tighter export controls put at risk? Will CEO Jensen Huang's
departure unravel Nvidia's circular financing strategy and the broader AI
investment boom? Tomorrow's earnings call, led by interim CEO Colette
Kress, could provide crucial answers.
In any event, money appears to be rotating out of technology and into the
broader market. The S&P 500 equal-weight stock index has outperformed

its market-weight counterpart in recent weeks (chart). The former remains
near its recent record high, while the latter is down 5.1% from its record
high on October 5. The Nasdaq is 11.2% below its October 5 record high.
SOXX is down 35.3% from its June 22 record high. The market is holding
up remarkably well given the shellacking of the semiconductor stocks.

### Page 4

II. China Is Weakening
China's economy continues to lose steam, with domestic demand
particularly weak. Consumers are depressed. Deflation is mounting
because of excess production. Here’s more:
(1) Retail sales & production. Retail sales rose just 0.4% y/y in October.
Inflation-adjusted retail sales fell 0.4%, highlighting persistent weakness in
domestic demand. Both measures are hovering around zero, a sharp
contrast to the double-digit growth rates that prevailed before the

pandemic.
Chinese consumers remain deeply pessimistic, with the consumer
confidence index far below its historical average (chart). They've been hit

### Page 5

by a huge negative wealth effect in the property market. They've also
been losing money in the stock and gold markets this year.
Industrial production rose 4.8% y/y in October despite declining real retail
sales, highlighting the imbalance between China's manufacturing output
and domestic consumption that pushes Chinese producers to dump more
goods onto global markets (chart).
Automobile production remains near record highs at an annualized 27.9
million vehicles, despite weak domestic demand (chart). Total domestic

passenger vehicle retail sales over the trailing 12-month period through
October came to roughly 19.5 million units, down about 20% y/y. The glut
has been exported.

### Page 6

(2) Inflation. China's inflation data further confirm weak domestic demand
and excess production. Headline CPI rose just 0.7% y/y in October, while
prices excluding food and energy declined 0.4% y/y (chart).
The prolonged property downturn is a major drag on Chinese consumers.
New house prices across 70 cities fell 2.4% y/y in October (chart). New
house prices have been falling since 2022.

### Page 7

Financial markets reflect these challenges. Chinese real estate stocks
remain deeply depressed, with the Shenzhen Real Estate Index near
levels last seen during the global financial crisis (chart). The CSI 300 has
been volatile and trendless for years.
(3) Trade. China's manufacturing overcapacity is increasingly spilling into
global markets. Merchandise exports have surged to a record $4.2 trillion
annualized, far outpacing imports of $3.1 trillion (chart).

### Page 8

As a result, China's trade surplus reached a record $1.27 trillion over the
past 12 months (chart).
China's crude oil imports, adjusted for Brent prices, are flattening after
decades of growth (chart). That's largely because more Chinese are
driving electric vehicles. This development was weighing on global oil
prices until the war in the Middle East boosted them this year.

### Page 9

China's exporters are increasingly looking beyond the US, with shipments
to Europe surging to nearly $890 billion over the past year, almost twice
the $463 billion shipped to America (chart).
Exports to Vietnam have also soared, to a record $250 billion, alongside
strong growth in shipments to South Korea and India (chart).

### Page 10

(4) Money & credit. China's credit boom has left its banking system with
enormous outstanding loans of $41.9 trillion, almost three times the $14.2
trillion outstanding at US banks (chart).
Bank loan growth continues to slow, with bank loans and M2 growth rates
in clear downward trends (chart).

### Page 11

Weak bank credit growth reflects the broader economic malaise, even as
Chinese interest rates remain exceptionally low (chart).
The People's Bank of China continues accumulating gold, reflecting its
efforts to diversify official reserves (chart).

### Page 12

clause.

## Document 329: Downgrade of global equities to a Core allocation

Source PDF: all report pdf/Downgrade of global equities to a Core allocation.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Market Watch)
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Downgrade of global 
equities to a Core allocation  
Summary 
Event: We are downgrading global equities from Overweight 
to a Neutral/Core allocation. We continue to maintain a 
Neutral allocation to fixed income, Overweight on Gold and 
Underweight on Cash. There are more details in our Global 
Market Outlook publication on 30 October 2026. 
What does the equity downgrade mean?  
This downgrade implies a slightly lower allocation to global 
equities within a diversified portfolio. For our CIO Foundation 
Balanced portfolio, this would imply an equity allocation of 
52.5%, down from 53.2%. We continue to expect positive 
returns for global equities in the coming 6-12 months, 
though equity investors may need to recalibrate expectations 
lower, compared with the 15-20% annualised gains 
experienced in the last few years. Our capital market 
assumptions still suggest a mid-to-high single digit annual 
returns over the next few years, consistent with a transition 
to a mid-cycle bull market. Consensus estimates point to 
16% earnings growth for global equities in 2027, which 
should overcome various headwinds to deliver positive 
returns for equities over the next 12 months. Investors should 
thus, continue to maintain exposure to equities within a 
diversified portfolio. 
In addition, we see a changing playbook to prepare for 
higher volatility, more active management and greater 
diversification, as discussed below. 
What does it mean for regional equities 
positioning? 
Within a global equities portfolio, we remain Overweight US 
and Asia ex-Japan (AxJ) equities, which are expected to 
deliver the highest earnings growth in 2027 amongst the 
major regions. This growth is led by the technology sector, 
which carries the largest weight in both regions. Within the 
diversified equities portfolio, we maintain Core allocations to

Euro area equities, which offer lower valuations, and Japan 
equities, which is backed by a reflating economy. We are 
Underweight UK equities, where the sector composition 
offers lower growth prospects in 2027. Within the AxJ region, 
we are Overweight Taiwan equities, with attractive structural 
growth driven by its semiconductor industry. We are also 
Overweight India equities, which offers diversified non-tech 
growth drivers. Taiwan and India equities both generate 
attractive returns on equity. We maintain Core allocations to 
China equities, with low valuations, and Korea equities, with 
explosive earnings growth in its semiconductors industry, but 
volatile price movements. We are Underweight ASEAN, 
which offers relatively lower earnings growth. 
What are the implications for equity sectors? 
We are Overweight these sectors globally: US 
information technology, US healthcare, US financials, US 
materials, Europe ex-UK financials, and Japan 
financials. 
A framework we use is to break the market into four key 
sector building blocks. Tech is the largest block, 
encompassing the Information Technology, Communication 
Services and Consumer Discretionary sectors. Put 
differently, the segment spans five major industries: 
semiconductors, hardware, internet, software, and IT 
services. Within the US market, we expect this segment to 
deliver c.30% earnings growth in 2027, extending the strong 
momentum seen in 2026. 
Financials and Real Estate are the next building block, 
comprising these two sectors. We expect this segment to 
continue delivering low-teens earnings growth in 2027, 
supported by resilient economic activity and healthy credit 
trends. 
Resources include companies across four sectors: 
Industrials, Materials, Energy, and Utilities. We expect the 
segment to generate 10-15% earnings growth in 2027, 
although Energy is likely to remain the largest drag on overall 
growth due to tougher comparisons and softer energy price 
assumptions for 2027. 
Healthcare & Others consists primarily of Healthcare and 
Consumer Staples. We expect mid-teen earnings growth for 
the segment in 2027, with a rebound in healthcare earnings 
representing a potential upside surprise. 
Our Overweight sector exposures are drawn from each of the 
four key blocks above. Unlike the last few years, with 
concentrated gains from Tech providing market leadership, 
we see our downgrade in global equities also reflecting a 
broadening in market leadership towards each of the other 
sector blocks apart from Tech.

As structural themes such as AI transition from the rapid 
buildout phase towards monetisation, diversified exposure 
across sectors and regions should become increasingly 
important. 
What are the implications for equity 
opportunistic ideas? 
We have opportunistic ideas on Global High-Dividend 
equities, US healthcare, Euro area banks, MSCI 
Switzerland and Japan banks. The selection represents a 
more defensive stance currently, with an average beta of 
below 1 relative to global equities (MSCI AC World), but 
attractive drivers nonetheless, for each of the ideas.  
Global High-Dividend offers higher-income equity allocation 
with historically lower volatility than global equities. US 
healthcare is supported by wider adoption of obesity 
treatments, advances in oncology and innovative medical 
devices, that support earnings growth. Euro area banks 
continue to see steady earnings growth adding to their 
excess capital, which is being returned to shareholders via 
attractive dividend and share buyback yields. Japan banks 
continue to enjoy net margin expansion from BoJ policy 
normalisation, as well as corporate governance 
improvements. MSCI Switzerland is a newly initiated idea for 
investors seeking defensive exposure. Healthcare and 
consumer staples accounts for more than half the index 
weight, providing exposure to relatively resilient demand that 
is more insulated from economic cycles and AI trends. It also 
has a sizeable financial sector supported by steady wealth 
management growth and well-capitalised insurers with 
disciplined underwriting and resilient premium income. 
We see the case for more active management in a mid-
cycle transition, as higher market dispersion should create 
greater opportunities for active sector and stock selection, 
particularly as leadership within equities evolves. 
What are the implications of higher equity 
market volatility?  
A more uncertain political backdrop and potentially maturing 
growth rates in certain market segments may contribute to 
more frequent bouts of market volatility. Higher volatility 
presents an opportunity to use structured strategies to gain 
exposure to equities. Such strategies may be used to 
generate income or to gain exposure at a discounted entry 
price. Investors can also consider strategies that are 
structured to return principal or partial principal at maturity. 
What alternative strategies are suited to a 
regime of moderating equity market returns?  
Investors can consider alternative strategies such as hedge 
fund strategies or private markets, for those with the capacity

to accept some illiquidity.  
Equity long/short hedge fund strategies would retain growth 
participation with less market exposure. Fund managers buy 
companies they expect to outperform and short those they 
expect to underperform, seeking to earn the difference 
between the two, and benefit even as total equity market 
returns are moderating. 
Within private markets, private equity provides a means to 
higher returns with the effects of an illiquidity premium. 
Meanwhile, infrastructure offers differentiated exposure to 
structural growth opportunities, where contracted long-term 
revenues reduce dependence on equity-market sentiment. 
What are the implications on fixed income 
and FX markets? 
Bonds: This year’s surge in bond yields means bonds now 
offer a hedge against any equity market weakness. We 
believe the 3-7-year part of the US bond yield curve offers 
the best hedge against any equity sell-off caused by AI-
related growth worries. This part of the bond curve has priced 
in most Fed rate hike expectations, which could reverse if 
growth concerns return. Meanwhile, we favour US Treasury 
Inflation-Protected Securities (TIPS) as a hedge against any 
fuel price-led inflation scare.  
Credit: For corporate bonds, adopting a defensive stance in 
a volatile environment typically involves: i) increasing credit 
quality, favouring higher-rated issuers; and ii) moving up the 
capital structure, by reducing exposure to subordinated debt 
and favouring secured debt. From a sector perspective, 
large, diversified banks, utilities, and quasi-sovereigns tend 
to be more defensive, particularly relative to cyclical sectors, 
such as metals and mining, exploration, and technology.         
FX: We favour bearish AUD/JPY as a tactical hedge against 
any equity market correction. Review of 21 past S&P500 
correction sessions (2010-2025) shows AUD weakened 
against USD in 86% of cases, while JPY strengthened 
against USD in 67%, supporting our bearish AUD/JPY 
outlook during any equity market stress.

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
All state Housing Sentiment Indexes are at weak levels.
However, November saw a bigger decline in Western
Australia (–4.8%mth) which now has the weakest housing-
related sentiment index across the major states at just 80.9.
Queensland and South Australia moved in line with the

national index. Moves were notably milder in New South
Wales (–1.7%) and especially Victoria (–0.7%) where the state
index, at 86.6, is the least pessimistic of the major states.
The sub-group detail showed a more pronounced weakening in
housing-related sentiment amongst high income earners and
consumers aged over 55 with sentiment holding about steady
amongst low-income earners, albeit at weaker overall levels.
Our November consumer sentiment survey also included

two new regular questions on consumers’ property buying
and selling intentions over the next twelve months. These
questions will continue to be run every month.
Despite the broader pessimism around housing, a relatively
high number of consumers are planning to buy in 2027.
Responses show 12.8% of consumers plan to buy a new
home over the next year. Comparable figures from Westpac’s
Home Ownership survey going back to 2019 suggest this is a
relatively firm result, up slightly since the middle of the year,

on par with the start of 2025 and above the levels reported
during the COVID pandemic in 2020-21. The result suggests
there is significant ‘latent demand’ for home purchases in
Australia, and that many buyers are just waiting for the right
opportunity. The state detail shows intentions are particularly
high in Queensland where one in five consumers is reportedly
planning to buy in 2027.
Interestingly, the positive intentions are not just around home

purchase with 11.2% of consumers reporting that they plan
to buy an investment property over the next year. Again,
comparable figures from Westpac’s Home Ownership survey
show this is up a tick compared to May and only marginally
below the 11.5% reporting intentions to buy investment
properties back in January 2025.
BULLETIN
The Westpac Housing Sentiment Index fell 2.2% to 83.2 in
November from 85.3 in October.
The Index is a composite based on housing-related sentiment

measures from the Westpac-Melbourne Institute Consumer
Sentiment survey. It already appears regularly in Westpac’s
Housing Pulse reports but will now be published on a
monthly basis. The Index has proven to be an extremely useful
predictor of turning points in housing markets, both nationally
and across states. Westpac is also introducing two new
survey questions on consumers property buying and selling
intentions that will be run every month.
The November update shows housing-related sentiment

remains remain deeply pessimistic. At 85.3, the latest Index
read is in the bottom 3% of observations since 2005, when all
components first became available. That said, it is still above the
81.4 low in late 2022 and the 78.6 registered when the COVID
pandemic hit in 2020. Housing-related sentiment was markedly
weaker during the slump in the late 1980s/early 1990s.
Component-wise, the November sentiment decline centred on
a sharp downgrade to price expectations. As detailed below,

the Westpac Housing Sentiment Index composite is based
on four sub-indexes: one tracking assessments of ‘time to buy
a dwelling’; one tracking ‘consumer house price expectations’;
one tracking consumer expectations for unemployment and a
measure of risk aversion based on consumer assessments of
the ‘wisest place for savings’.
Consumer price expectations took a big step down in
November. The Westpac–Melbourne Institute Index of
House Price Expectations recorded a steep 13.4% drop

to 99.6, marking the second biggest monthly deterioration
since the COVID period and the first net negative (sub-100)
read since November 2022. The detailed responses show
consumers are now evenly split on the outlook, just over 38%
expecting more declines, 19% expecting no change and 38%
expecting a rise (5% reported “don’t know).
At least some consumers are starting to see lower prices as
a potential buying opportunity. The ‘time to buy a dwelling’

index rose 1.3% to 89.6 in November, to be up 1.3% on a year
ago as well but still at a very weak level overall, well below
the long run average of 114.
The wider economic backdrop was also less conducive for
housing-related sentiment. Consumers continue to report
a gradual rise in job-loss fears and risk aversion remains
elevated near historic highs. Both factors are likely to be
deterring prospective home buyers.
Housing sentiment sinks but buying intentions positive

“Despite the broader pessimism
around housing, a relatively high
number of consumers are planning
to buy ...”
Matthew Hassan
Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Conversely, just 8.2% of Australians are planning to sell a home 
over the next year. Comparable figures from Westpac’s Home 
Ownership survey suggest this is down significantly from 10.3% 
back in May and near the 7.7% low recorded at the start of 2024. 
On investment properties, 6.7% of all those surveyed reported 
plans to sell. However, only 23% of consumers reported 
owning an investment property – as a share of this sub-
group, the proportion planning to sell is just over 26%. While 
it is unclear whether this is relatively high (no comparable 
measures are available from Westpac’s Home Ownership 
survey), a third of this same sub-group reported an intention to 
buy an investment property over the next year, indicating that 
amongst investment property owners prospective buyers still 
outnumber prospective sellers. 
Overall, the consumer views on housing show a clear tension. 
On the one side, high interest rates, a continued slide in prices 
and unease about a riskier outlook for jobs and the wider 
economy are weighing heavily on housing-related sentiment. 
But against this, relatively few are planning to sell and there 
is a relatively high proportion of consumers looking to buy. 
This suggests that conditions could recover quite quickly if and 
when the macro-economic backdrop becomes less threatening 
and the market starts to stabilise.

About the Westpac Housing Sentiment Index 
The Westpac Housing Sentiment Index is a composite 
measure based on a weighted combination of four indexes 
from the Westpac–Melbourne Institute Consumer 
Sentiment survey. Two of these are ‘primary’ components 
with a higher weight that relate directly to consumer 
perceptions of housing market conditions: the Westpac–
Melbourne Institute ‘time to buy a dwelling’ index and the 
Westpac–Melbourne Institute House Price Expectations 
Index. The remaining ‘supplementary’ components, with 
lower weights, relate to consumer assessments of job 
security – the Westpac–Melbourne Institute Unemployment 
Expectations Index – and risk appetite – the Westpac Risk 
Aversion Index.
Each of these components is seasonally adjusted, converted 
to a consistent base and combined using fixed weights 
determined by historical regression analysis. Note that the 
house price expectations component is only available from 
2009 on – a re-weighted composite based on the remaining 
measures is used for earlier periods.  
The resulting composite measures provide significant insight 
into housing market conditions both nationally and at the 
individual state level. The national index has over 50yrs of 
history and a clear lead indicator relationship with a variety 
of housing market metrics. The index is particularly good at 
picking turning points in housing market turnover – correctly 
anticipating every major upswing and downturn since 1980 
with a lead of around three months (four once the timeliness 
of sentiment updates is included).

## Document 395: NZ labour market review chartpack, Q3 2026 (2026-11-19)

Source PDF: all report pdf/NZ labour market review chartpack, Q3 2026 (2026-11-19).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-19
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

LABOUR MARKET CHARTPACK
4 Nov 2026

### Page 2

Westpac New Zealand Economics
2
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us
Key points – September quarter 2026
• The September quarter surveys pointed to ongoing softness in labour market conditions. The unemployment rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7%, continuing an
almost five-year run of increases. The unemployment rate is now at its highest level since September 2015.
• There were mixed messages in terms of jobs growth: the Household Labour Force Survey reported a 0.1% fall in employment, while the employer-

focused Quarterly Employment Survey suggested a modest rise. The Monthly Employment Indicator had also pointed to a small rise in jobs.
• The fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a fall in the participation rate. These co-movements essentially reversed the surprisingly large
increases that we saw in the June quarter, which we had suggested at the time were more likely due to sampling error in the survey rather than a
genuine surge.
• We generally recommend focusing on the unemployment rate, which is less vulnerable to sampling errors – and the message here is one of softness. Our

best interpretation is that, to the extent that jobs are growing, it’s not fast enough to absorb the growth in the population.
• While the economy has returned to growth over the last year or so, hiring activity remains weak. This may be an ongoing legacy of employment trends in
previous years, when firms were relatively reluctant to lay people off during the slowdown, which then potentially left them overstaffed even as activity
started to pick up again. It’s not clear how long this effect can persist, though.

• Wage growth remains muted. Although cost-of-living pressures have increased on workers, the existing degree of spare capacity in the labour market
doesn’t give them much leverage to negotiate larger pay increases.
• Overall, we judge these results to be weaker than the RBNZ expected, in particular the rise in the unemployment rate. This data supports the views of
those on the policy committee who were less convinced in October about the potential for second-round inflation pressures in the economy.

### Page 3

Westpac New Zealand Economics
3
Employment
• The number of people employed fell by 0.1% in the September quarter, according to the
Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS). Previous quarters were revised down a little,
reflecting downward revisions to the working-age population estimates. Annual employment
growth slowed from 0.9% to 0.7%.
• This was substantially softer than our forecast of a 0.5% rise for the quarter. Our forecast
was based on the Monthly Employment Indicator (MEI), a comprehensive measure that is

drawn from income tax data. While there are some differences in coverage for these two
measures, any significant divergence between the two is most likely to reflect sampling error
in the HLFS.
• Meanwhile, the employer-focused Quarterly Employment Survey (QES) recorded a 0.1% rise
in the number of filled jobs and a 0.6% rise in hours paid. (This survey is of course vulnerable
to sampling error as well.)
• We’re most inclined towards the MEI measure of employment, given the breadth of its

coverage. But our best interpretation is that, to the extent that jobs are growing, it’s not
yet fast enough to absorb the growth in the adult population – hence the further rise in the
unemployment rate.

Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us

### Page 4

Westpac New Zealand Economics
4
Labour force participation
• The surprising fall in HLFS employment was accompanied by a drop in the participation rate
from 70.6% to 70.4%.
• These co-movements are effectively a reversal of the surprisingly large increases that we
saw in the June quarter, which we noted at the time were suggestive of sampling error in the
survey. Any such errors largely wash out when it comes to the unemployment rate, however,
which is why we recommend focusing on this measure.

• Setting aside the usual quarterly volatility, labour force participation remains caught
between two competing forces. One is cyclical weakness, especially among younger people,
who are finding it difficult to break into a “low-fire, low-hire” labour market. The teen
unemployment rate of around 25% is a record high going back to the start of the survey
in 1986.
• The other force is rising participation among the over-60s (who also make up a growing

share of the population). Kiwis have had a growing propensity to work beyond the NZ Super
age, as there is no penalty for doing so. It’s unclear to what extent this can be considered
an increase in the pool of available workers: older workers tend to have low mobility
between jobs, and if they find themselves out of work are more likely to leave the labour
force altogether.

### Page 5

Westpac New Zealand Economics
5
Unemployment and underutilisation
• The unemployment rate rose from 5.6% to 5.7% in the September quarter. This was the
highest rate since September 2015.
• While there are other indicators of spare capacity in the labour market, we’re not convinced
that there is additional information to draw from them this time.
• The broader underutilisation measure, which includes discouraged jobseekers and part-
time workers wanting more hours, was steady at 13.8% after having risen sharply from

12.9% last quarter. This measure is both higher (by definition) and more variable than the
unemployment rate, but tells a similar story in that it’s around an 11-year high.
• There was a marked 1.6% fall in the number of hours worked (although ‘usual’ hours were
down just 0.4%). However, we suspect this was largely driven by Matariki – a floating holiday
that won’t be captured in the seasonal adjustment.
• This may also account for the 2.5% rise in part-time employment, against a 0.7% fall in

full-time employment. Some workers may have been pushed into the part-time category
(less than 30 hours a week) simply by virtue of only working four days during the
survey week.

Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us

### Page 6

Westpac New Zealand Economics
6
Hiring
• Business confidence surveys have shown a lift in hiring intentions in recent months, as firms
have shaken off the initial shock of the Iran conflict and the surge in fuel prices.
• However, those stated intentions aren’t obviously turning into reality yet. Job advertisements
were down about 1% for the September quarter in seasonally adjusted terms, and they
remain well below pre-Covid levels. Job ads will capture ‘churn’ within existing roles as well

as new job creation, but both of these would be associated with a strong labour market.
• With the working-age population growing at the same time, the result was a modest fall in
the vacancy rate, one of the RBNZ’s highest-ranked measures of labour market tightness.
• We estimate that there are around 1.9 people unemployed for every job vacancy, up from
around 1.8 people this time last year.
• Firms have told us that they were reluctant to lay off workers during the economic downturn

in 2023-24, as they were wary of the extreme labour shortages that had developed in the
previous boom years. As a result, they may find themselves still overstaffed even as demand
has started to pick up again, and haven’t needed to return to hiring yet.

### Page 7

Westpac New Zealand Economics
7
Population and migration
• The working-age population grew by around 1.0% over the last year, compared to a peak of
3.3% growth over 2023. Jobs growth (based on the less volatile MEI measure) is still lagging
behind population growth on an annual basis.
• The pool of potential workers is generally becoming older, with the Baby Boomers
increasingly reaching pension age and beyond, and with migrant arrivals tending to be an

older cohort than we saw pre-Covid – more in their 30s and 40s than their 20s.
• Net migration flows have started to pick up from their lows since late 2025. Departures of
New Zealanders have come down from their highs, though they remain above pre-Covid
levels, as the relative appeal of the Australian jobs market has narrowed.
• Foreign arrivals have picked up to some degree, although the growth appears to have been
mostly driven by students rather than those on work visas.

### Page 8

Westpac New Zealand Economics
8
Sectoral and regional trends
• Jobs growth has been relatively strong in government-connected sectors such as healthcare
and education, as well as in local and central government administration.
• Mining and utilities have seen strong growth in percentage terms, but neither of these
sectors are big employers in absolute terms.
• The construction sector is now starting to see a lift in employment, after a steep decline

over 2024-25. Residential building consents have risen strongly over the last year or so,
pointing to a growing pipeline of work for the homebuilding industry.
• Sectors that are connected to international tourism, such as transport and recreational
services, had previously seen strong employment growth, but this appears to have flattened
out in recent months.
• Employment trends have generally been more positive in the South Island than the North,

which likely reflects the greater influence of the agricultural sector – while not a large
employer in itself, farmers have benefited from high export prices and their spending has
flowed into other sectors.

Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us

### Page 9

Westpac New Zealand Economics
9
Wages
• The Labour Cost Index (LCI) rose by 0.5% in the September quarter. Annual growth
remained around the lowest since 2021.
• While the overall result was in line with our forecast, the details were a little weaker than
we expected, with less contribution from private sector wages (up 0.5%) and a surprisingly
large 0.7% increase in the public sector.
• The distribution of pay rates has returned to pre-Covid trends. Only 44% of roles saw a

pay rise over the last year; of those roles that did see an increase, the average size has
converged on the 2-3% range.
• The LCI is not a measure of what workers actually receive – it reflects the cost to businesses
for a given unit of labour – and hence shouldn’t be compared to the inflation rate. A more
suitable measure is the analytical unadjusted LCI, which includes pay rises that are related
to productivity gains. This measure slowed from 3.1% to 2.9% on an annual basis, the lowest

since March 2021.
• There was a marked pickup in the proportion of firms reporting cost-of-living adjustments as
the main reason for pay increases. However, the size of those increases varied greatly, with
many of them below the rate of CPI inflation.
• This seems to reflect a shift in the justification for pay rises, rather than an overall increase in
wage pressures – firms are more likely to cite cost-of-living adjustments because they’re less
likely to cite demand-side pressures.

### Page 10

Westpac New Zealand Economics
10
International comparisons
• New Zealand’s unemployment rate is the 18th lowest in the OECD (out of 38 countries). This
ranking has been broadly unchanged over the last year or so.
• Notably, New Zealand’s unemployment rate is substantially above Australia’s, whereas
historically it has been lower on average. This shift in relative job opportunities has been a
key attractor for New Zealanders over the last couple of years – as it was in the early 2010s

when the Australian economy was benefiting from a boom in mining construction.
• However, that gap between unemployment rates is no longer widening, with the Australian
jobs market starting to soften this year as the RBA has raised interest rates to rein in inflation
(which was already running too high before the Iran conflict and the surge in oil prices). We
expect Australia’s unemployment to rise further over the year ahead.
• New Zealand has the 7th highest employment rate in the OECD (down from 6th last quarter).

We tend to have high rates of labour force participation for 15-64 year olds, and particularly
high participation for the over-65s, as the lack of means testing for NZ Superannuation
means there is no financial disincentive to working beyond pension age.

## Document 414: September CPI_ Inflation accelerating in the run up to Christmas (2026-11-18)

Source PDF: all report pdf/September CPI_ Inflation accelerating in the run up to Christmas (2026-11-18).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 18
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#inflation #australia #rba #economicbulletinsaustralia #inflation
September CPI: Inflation accelerating in the run up to Christmas
By Justin Smirk & Neha Sharma
16:08 October 28 2026 Share
September Monthly CPI: Headline 0.8%mth/4.3%yr, Trimmed Mean 0.3.%mth/3.6%yr.
Market services ex volatile 1.6%mth/4.0%yr. September Quarter CPI: Headline
1.3%qtr/3.9%yr; Trimmed Mean 1.0%qtr/3.7%yr.
The CPI rose 0.8% in the September month, stronger than Westpac’s 0.6% estimate and the

market’s expectation of 0.7%. The annual pace picked up from 4.0%yr to 4.3%yr, The seasonally
adjusted CPI rose 0.6% in September highlighting some positive seasonality in the month.

### Page 2

Key factors driving the headline monthly result were: automotive fuels (9.4%mth); domestic holiday
travel & accommodation (8.0%mth); international holiday travel & accommodation (7.2%mth); and
property rates & charges (5.5%mth). There were modest offsets from: women’s garments (–
3.1%mth); household textiles (–6.6%mth); and accessories (–4.1%mth).
The Trimmed Mean (TM) gained 0.3% in September, spot on both Westpac and market expectations
with the annual pace lifting from 3.5%yr (revised from 3.6%yr) to 3.6%yr. The six-month annualised

pace is now 4.0%yr the fastest in the short history of this series.
Headline CPI lifted 1.3% in the September quarter compared to an expectation of 1.3% by Westpac
and 1.2% by the market. This saw the annual pace hold at 3.9%yr.
The RBA’s key underlying inflation measure, the quarterly TM, lifted 1.0% vs an expectation of
0.9% by Westpac and market estimate of 1.0%. The annual pace lifted from 3.6%yr to 3.7%yr
with the six-month annualised pace accelerating to 3.7%yr from 3.4%yr.

Overall, the estimates come in broadly as expected so we see minimal risk to our current December
quarter forecast of 1.3%qtr for the headline CPI. Softer housing inflation does present a very modest
downside risk to our 0.9%qtr for the TM but there are offsetting gains elsewhere.
The September CPI update revealed underlying inflationary pressures continuing even while a
moderation in housing inflation (dwellings in particular) is unfolding slightly faster than we had

anticipated. As such, the near-term risk for inflation remains to the upside. The RBA will remain alert
to these near-term inflationary risks and as so, we hold to the view that the RBA will raise rates again
at the November meeting.

### Page 3

Month of September CPI
The monthly CPI rose 0.8%mth in September, above both our expectation of a 0.6%mth increase and
the market forecast of 0.7%mth. This lifted the annual pace by 0.3ppts to 4.3%yr. On a seasonally
adjusted basis, the CPI increased 0.6%mth.
The key contributors to the monthly increase were: automotive fuel (9.4%mth, +0.3ppts); and holiday
travel & accommodation (7.6%mth, +0.5ppts), alongside a smaller contribution from the annual reset in

property rates (5.5%mth, +0.1ppt). There was no single major offset. Instead, several components
across household furnishings and clothing & footwear recorded modest declines, together subtracting a
little over 0.1ppt from the monthly result.
The monthly trimmed mean (TM) rose 0.3%mth, in line with both our and the market’s expectations.
The annual pace edged up to 3.6%yr, although the August outcome was revised down to 3.5%yr.
Underlying inflation has remained broadly stuck in the 3.5–3.6%yr range since May. On a 3m/3m basis,

the monthly TM was 1.1%.

### Page 4

Market services excluding volatile items and holiday travel were flat in the month, with annual inflation
easing to 3.8%yr, its softest pace since November 2025. On a seasonally adjusted basis, monthly
inflation moderated across most components in this measure, with telecommunications services, rents
and other financial services the key exceptions. However, all but two components are still recording
annual price growth above 3%, suggesting that disinflation across market services remains slow and

relatively narrow. The persistence of these underlying price pressures is likely to remain a concern for
the RBA.

### Page 5

Housing inflation was notably softer in the month, rising 0.3%mth to be 5.2% higher over the year.
Rents increased 0.2%mth, slightly below our expectation for a 0.3%mth rise. The biannual indexation
of Commonwealth Rent Assistance occurred late in the month, weighing on the September outcome,
with the remainder of the impact expected to flow through in October. The ABS noted that rents would
have risen 0.3%mth in the absence of the measure, while on a seasonally adjusted basis rents

increased 0.4%mth.
New dwelling inflation was flat in the month, the softest reading since May 2025. Monthly inflation has
now slowed in each successive month since May 2026, suggesting homebuilders’ pricing power is
becoming increasingly constrained. The ABS noted that some project home builders raised base prices
this month only to be offset by some builders increasing discounts and promotional offers in response
to slowing demand. The annual pace eased to 4.7%yr. Dwelling repair costs were also a downside

surprise, falling 0.1%mth, their first decline since February 2025, although prices remain 3.4% higher
over the year. Some of this softness was offset by a firmer-than-expected increase in property rates
and charges, which rose 5.6%mth.
Recently, we have also focused on audio, visual and computing given the potential for spillovers from
strong global demand for semiconductors and other computing resources associated with the AI build
out. Prices for both equipment and services came in weaker than August, with equipment prices falling

1.1%mth, while services rose 0.3%mth. Despite this, annual price growth remains relatively high for
these categories, running at 4.6% and 4.9% respectively. Some of this pressure might also be showing

### Page 6

through in gaming, toys and hobbies, which is running at 5.2%mth/15.5%yr.
Overall, the breadth of inflation moderated for a second consecutive month. The share of the basket
running above 3%yr fell from 40% to 35%, meanwhile the share below 2%yr lifted from 27% to 32%.
Our replication of the San Francisco Fed’s inflation shock momentum served as a good guide to the
quarterly TM. With the full monthly reads in for the September quarter in, this measure does signal
some persistence/upside pressures for the quarterly TM going into the coming quarters. It is worth
cautioning that this measure will be impacted as the ABS refines its seasonal adjustment process for
the monthly indexes.

Compared to our expectations, the key surprises were:
Upside surprises
Downside surprises
Fruit & vegetables was the key upside surprise, 0.6%mth against our expectations for 3.7%mth fall.
Holiday travel & accommodation also surprised to the upside, following a run of downside surprises
since early this year. It rose 7.6%mth against our expectations for a 4.4%mth lift. 
Automotive fuel rose 9.4%mth against expectations of an 8.3%mth lift.

### Page 7

September Quarter CPI
Clothing & footwear was the key downside surprise with prices falling 2.7%mth against our
expectations for a 0.2%mth fall. The misses were broad-based: garments (–2.3%mth vs –2.0%mth);
footwear (–2.5%mth vs 1.8%mth); and accessories & clothing services (–4.1%mth vs 2.8%mth).
Household textiles declined 6.6%mth against our expectations for a 3.2%mth fall. Furniture &
furnishings rose by less-than-expected 0.4%mth vs 1.6%mth. Household appliances, utensils and

tools also came in softer, –3.2%mth vs –2.4%mth.
Dairy prices fell 1.2%mth against our expectations for a 0.1%mth lift.

### Page 8

The September quarter CPI printed spot on our expectation of 1.3%. The key factor driving this
increase was the slightly stronger surge in auto fuel (7.8%, +0.27ppt) and holiday travel &
accommodation (2.7%qtr, +0.16ppt) being offset by a slightly softer than expected increase in housing
(1.1%qtr, +0.24ppt) with rents and dwellings both increasing 1.0% in the quarter and utilities lifting
1.2%. Food was also slightly stronger than expected (0.7%qtr, +0.12ppt) and while not significant to the
overall result it is interesting to track the ongoing rise in motor vehicle prices (0.3%qtr) despite a very
competitive market with Chinese manufacturers rapidly gaining market share over legacy brands.

It is worth noting that food was stronger than we expected (0.7% vs 0.5%) with a robust 1.7% increase
in restaurant meals and a solid 1.2% increase in take away & fast foods outweighing the –0.4% decline
in fresh fruit & vegetables and meat & seafoods. Overall, market services ex volatile prices increased a
very solid 1.2%qtr but with the annual pace easing to 3.2%yr from 3.36%yr.
Outside of housing the other main downside surprise in the quarter was the –0.6% fall in clothing &
footwear (+0.3% expected) with prices falling across just about all categories of garments, footwear
and accessories.
September Quarter Trimmed Mean

### Page 9

Recall that the TM is calculated from seasonally adjust consumption expenditure classes so any
numbers quoted here may not be the same as the original estimates quoted from the CPI. The TM gain
of 1.0% in the quarter was slightly stronger than we had expected even though dwellings (which have a
weight of almost 11% in the TM) was a touch softer at 1.0% compared to the 1.1% we had pencilled in.
Rents (1.0%, 10% weight) also made a solid contribution as did restaurant meals (1.7%qr, 6% weight),

take away & fast food (1.2%, 4% weight), other financial service (1.0%, 6% weight). Domestic travel
was in the TM (1.0%, 4% weight) as was medical & hospital service (1.0%, weight 5%). The range of
the TM was from 0.0% (motor vehicles) to 1.7% (beer).
What does this mean for our December quarter forecast and into 2027?
We are working though the monthly results to see what changes they may imply for individual
component inflationary momentum. Our preliminary analysis suggests that the softer trend in dwellings

is not enough, on its own, to change our Q4 CPI forecast of 1.3%. It does, however, suggest that if all
else held equal (which is not the case as there is likely to be some offsetting stronger gains elsewhere)
the moderation in dwelling inflation presents a very modest downside risk our Q4 TM estimate of 0.9%.
We are still working the numbers and will publish an updated inflation profile as soon as we can. In
addition, the overall momentum in underlying inflationary pressures suggest the downside risk to the

TM from dwellings is more likely to appear in 2027 than in the last quarter of 2026.

鍾沛寰 / IC 設計
